\title{PPR Acquisition Policy Under Nonstationarity --- October 28,
2026}
\author{}
\date{}

% Tighten the gap between the title block and the first line of text.
% Make this MORE negative to tighten further, less negative to loosen.
\vspace{-2\baselineskip}


\emph{Results generated 2026-10-20.}

\section{Project Overview}\label{project-overview}

This project asks whether accounting for non-stationarity leads to
better land-acquisition decisions for waterfowl conservation in the U.S.
Prairie Pothole Region (PPR) under different climate scenarios. It
builds three decision models and compares their performance across
climate-change scenarios and a stationary null.

The three models acquire land period by period (decadal steps,
2020--2100) under a budget constraint:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Greedy} --- status-quo heuristic: rank parcels by current
  benefit-to-cost ratio, buy down the list. No foresight.
\item
  \textbf{Myopic ILP} --- re-optimizes each decade but freezes current
  benefit and hazard and assumes they persist (a fabricated stationary
  future); implements the current decade, then re-solves.
\item
  \textbf{Rolling-horizon ILP} --- same structure, but uses the true
  projected trajectories. The foresighted benchmark. Uses Gurobi solver
  (academic license).
\end{itemize}

The spatial unit is the \textbf{EAU} (Equal Area Unit,
\textasciitilde342 km²). After dropping 4 Wetland Management Districts,
the analysis uses \textbf{879 EAUs across 20 WMDs}, over \textbf{9
decadal periods} and \textbf{5 scenarios} (RCP 4.5/8.5 × wet/dry, plus a
stationary null).

\section{Data Sources}\label{data-sources}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Benefit}: McKenna, Owen P., et al.~``Conservation enhances
  resilience of the United States Duck Factory as environmental shifts
  may diminish landscape-scale waterfowl breeding capacity.'' BioScience
  (2026): biag091.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    projects breeding duck pairs over RCP 4.5/8.5 × wet/dry scenarios.
  \item
    linear interpolation to get from obs/mid-century/end of century to
    decadal time steps.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Cost}: Nolte, Christoph. ``High-resolution land value maps
  reveal underestimation of conservation costs in the United States.''.
  Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences. 117.47 (2020):
  29577-29583.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    assume 2\% unfiform annual increase
  \item
    note no emissions or precip scenarios.
  \end{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Risk}: FOREsce (derived). Sohl, Terry L., et al.~``Spatially
  explicit land-use and land-cover scenarios for the Great Plains of the
  United States.'' Agriculture, Ecosystems \& Environment 153 (2012):
  1-15.

  \begin{itemize}
  \tightlist
  \item
    GCAM reference scenario
  \item
    calculated as gross suitable habitat loss per time step.
  \item
    note this has RCP 4.5 \& 8.5 scenarios, but no precip scenario.
  \end{itemize}
\end{itemize}

\section{Model Formulation}\label{model-formulation}

\textbf{Decision model.} Let \(i \in \mathcal{I}\) index EAUs
(\(|\mathcal{I}| = 879\)) and \(t \in \{0, \dots, T\}\) index decades (0
= 2020, \(T = 8\) = 2100). For EAU \(i\) in period \(t\), let
\(b_{i,t}\) be projected breeding duck pairs under a given scenario,
\(\lambda_{i,t}\) the per-period probability that its suitable habitat
is converted, and \(c_{i,t}\) the cost of acquisition (land value per
hectare × EAU area, inflated at 2\% per year).

\emph{Survival.} If EAU \(i\) is not acquired, the probability that it
remains unconverted through period \(t\) is

\[S_{i,0} = 1, \qquad S_{i,t} = \prod_{s=0}^{t-1} \left(1 - \lambda_{i,s}\right), \quad t \ge 1.\]

where for a given EAU, its per-decade survival probability is defined as
1-\(\lambda_{i,t}\) (i.e., the conversion hazard risk) and the
cumulative survival probability in time \(t\) is the product of each
decade's survival probability from 2020 through the decade just before
\(t\) (\(t\)-1).

\emph{Acquisition value.} The conservation value of acquiring an EAU is
calculated as the expected loss its acquisition prevents. Acquiring EAU
\(i\) in period \(\tau\) protects its benefit with certainty from
\(\tau\) onward.

\[V_{i,\tau} = \sum_{t=\tau}^{T} \delta^{t}\, b_{i,t} \left(1 - S_{i,t}\right),\]
where \(V_{i,\tau}\) is the value of acquiring parcel \(i\) in period
\(\tau\), expressed as the total time-discounted sum of duck-pairs
across the landscape from \(\tau\) through 2100; \(\delta\) is the
decadal discount factor (0.95); \(b_{i,t}\) is the projected benefit
(duck pairs) in parcel \(i\) in period \(t\) under a given scenario; and
\(S_{i,t}\) is the probability that parcel \(i\) survives (i.e., remains
unconverted) through period \(t\) if it is not acquired, so that
\(1 - S_{i,t}\) is the probability of conversion. Total landscape value
under any acquisition schedule equals the value of doing nothing plus
\(\sum_i V_{i,\tau_i}\) (i.e., the sum of acquisition value), summed
over all acquired parcel.

All unacquired parcels are treated as available (i.e.~subject to
conversion risk) at each re-solve; conversion does not remove parcels
from the set, but instead enters \textbf{only} through the weighting in
the objective. Acquisition removes this weight and counts the full
(time-discounted) value; unacquired parcels retain only their
survival-discounted value.

\emph{Optimization.} Let \(x_{i,t} \in \{0,1\}\) equal 1 if EAU \(i\) is
acquired in period \(t\), and let \(\mathcal{A}_\tau\) be the set of
EAUs not yet acquired at the start of period \(\tau\). At each decision
period \(\tau\), the ILP policies solve

\[\max_{x} \; \sum_{i \in \mathcal{A}_\tau} \sum_{t=\tau}^{T} \hat{V}_{i,t}\, x_{i,t}\]

subject to

\[\begin{aligned}
\sum_{t=\tau}^{T} x_{i,t} &\le 1 && \forall\, i \in \mathcal{A}_\tau && \text{(each EAU acquired at most once)} \\
\sum_{i \in \mathcal{A}_\tau} c_{i,t}\, x_{i,t} &\le B_t && \forall\, t = \tau, \dots, T && \text{(per-period budget)} \\
x_{i,t} &\in \{0,1\}.
\end{aligned}\]

Only the period-\(\tau\) decisions are carried out. Then
\(\mathcal{A}_{\tau+1} = \mathcal{A}_\tau \setminus \{i : x_{i,\tau} = 1\}\)
and the problem is solved again. Conversion does not remove parcels from
\(\mathcal{A}_\tau\); it enters only through the weight
\((1 - S_{i,t})\).

The policies differ only in \(\hat{V}\). For the rolling-horizon policy,
\(\hat{V}_{i,t} = V_{i,t}\), computed from the projected trajectories of
\(b\) and \(\lambda\). For the myopic policy, benefit and hazard are
held at their period-\(\tau\) values for the whole horizon, so the
coefficients are recomputed at every solve:

\[\hat{V}^{(\tau)}_{i,t} = \sum_{s=t}^{T} \delta^{s}\, b_{i,\tau} \left[1 - \left(1 - \lambda_{i,\tau}\right)^{s}\right].\]

\emph{Budget.} The budget for each period is

\[B_t = k \cdot \operatorname*{median}_{i \in \mathcal{I}} \left(c_{i,t}\right), \qquad k = 5 \;\; (\text{sensitivity: } k \in \{2, 10\}).\]

Because all costs inflate at the same rate, \(B_t\) is constant in real
terms. The budget cannot be carried forward or borrowed against. The
greedy policy is held to the same \(B_t\): in each period it acquires
available EAUs in decreasing order of \(b_{i,t}/c_{i,t}\) until no
remaining EAU fits within what is left of \(B_t\).

\newpage{}

\section{Results}\label{results}

\subsection{Descriptive Summary}\label{descriptive-summary}

\subsubsection{Duck distribution}\label{duck-distribution}

Consistent with other studies (e.g., McKenna et al, 2026), duck
abundance is higher in the western portion of the PPR than the eastern
portion. This overall distribution pattern continues through 2100 (Fig.
1). In all scenarios, duck abundance is predicted to decline across the
PPR by 2100, although wet scenarios fare better than dry scenarios (Fig.
2). Note that this figure was produced after a spatial join error was
corrected.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/map_benefit_2020_2100.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-benefit-map}\textbf{Duck-pair abundance across the
PPR: 2020 baseline vs.~2100 under RCP 8.5 dry.} The 2020 panel is the
shared cross-scenario baseline common to all five scenarios --- only the
2100 panel is scenario-specific. One tile = one EAU.}

\end{figure}%

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/fig_benefit_ribbon.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-benefit-ribbon}\textbf{Per-EAU duck-pair abundance
to 2100, by scenario.} Solid line = median across EAUs; dark band =
interquartile range; light band = 10th--90th percentile. Bands show
spatial variability across EAUs within a given scenario-year.}

\end{figure}%

\newpage{}

\subsubsection{Risk}\label{risk}

Risk of conversion across all EAUs is heavily skewed, with the vast
majority of EAUs showing a nearly zero per period conversion risk; the
maximum per period conversion risk for a given EAU is
\textasciitilde15\%. There is a sharp drop in per period conversion risk
in 2050, which is a function of the FOREsce model: cropland conversion
is frontloaded through 2050, at which point conversions to cropland
decline. Note that this metric has been corrected from net habitat loss
to gross habitat loss.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/fig_risk_box.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-risk-box}\textbf{Per-period conversion risk across
EAUs, by decade and emissions scenario.} Box = median and IQR; whiskers
= 1.5 × IQR; horizontal tick = maximum; points = individual EAUs. The
number above each box is the \% of EAUs sitting at the background
conversion floor.}

\end{figure}%

\newpage{}

\subsubsection{Cost}\label{cost}

Acquisition cost per hectare in USD 2020. High cost parcels are
concentrated in the eastern portion of the PPR, consistent with other
analyses (e.g., Ando and Mallory, 2012).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.85\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/map_cost_binned.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cost-map}\textbf{Acquisition cost per hectare, 2020
USD inflated from 2017.} Raw dollars, no log transform, grouped into six
equal-count classes.}

\end{figure}%

\newpage{}

\subsection{Model results}\label{model-results}

\subsection{Relative performance of acquisition
policies}\label{relative-performance-of-acquisition-policies}

The major findings of this study compare the difference between total
benefit secured across the landscape (2020-2100) under the rolling ILP
policy as compared to the greedy or myopic policy. As expected, the
rolling policy outperforms both the myopic and the greedy policy; the
performance gap between the rolling policy and the greedy policy is
larger than the rolling-myopic gap. Rolling provides a 18-20\%
improvement over myopic, and a \textasciitilde49-57\% improvement over
greedy. Under stationarity, there is virtually no difference (as
expected, since a stationary environment provides no penalty for
policies without foresight). The rolling policy outperforms the myopic
and greedy policies under all climate scenarios, consistent with our
expectations that an acquisition policy with foresight should outperform
a policy with no or limited foresight under any degree of
non-stationarity.

\textbf{My interpretation: A non-stationary, climate-aware acquisition
policy could deliver a \textasciitilde50\% improvement in conservation
outcomes as compared to the greedy (i.e., status quo) acquisition policy
in use today, potentially protecting an additional 37,000-69,000 duck
pairs by 2100}.

\global\setlength{\arrayrulewidth}{\Oldarrayrulewidth}

\global\setlength{\tabcolsep}{\Oldtabcolsep}

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

\newpage{}

Another way of describing this is to ask how short of optimal the greedy
and myopic policies fall. Here, the rolling policy is used as the
provably near-optimal benchmark (MIP gap 0.01\% across all rolling
solves), and performance gaps are expressed as a \% of rolling. The
myopic policy falls \textasciitilde15-17\% short of rolling, and the
greedy policy falls \textasciitilde33-36\% short of rolling.

\textbf{My interpretation: Current use of acquisition policies that
assume environmental stationarity fall short of an optimal acquisition
policy by \textasciitilde30\%, failing to secure an additional
37,000-69,000 potential duck pairs by 2100}.

\subsection{Relative performance over
time}\label{relative-performance-over-time}

This graph shows how the gap between the rolling policy and the
myopic/greedy policies grows over time. This is presented as net changes
in duck pairs, using the rolling policy as a benchmark (value added gap
for rolling = 0). As expected, the cumulative gap in performance between
rolling and greedy/myopic grows over time (negative values only); and
greedy is substantially worse than myopic (red always \textless{}
yellow).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/overtime_gap.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-overtime}\textbf{Cumulative performance relative to
the rolling policy.} Discounted duck-pairs secured by the greedy and
myopic policies minus those secured by rolling, cumulative to 2100, by
climate scenario. Rolling is the near-optimal benchmark (0); MIP gap
\textasciitilde0.01\%.}

\end{figure}%

\newpage{}

\subsection{Acquisition value over
baseline}\label{acquisition-value-over-baseline}

This table compares the total benefit secured across the landscape
(2020-2100) through the three acquisition policies against a
``do-nothing'' baseline scenario in which no parcels are acquired. All
acquisition policies outperform the do-nothing baseline under climate
change scenarios; there is no improvement under assumptions of
stationarity (as expected: stationarity freezes conversion risk to
effectively zero).

Even a greedy acquisition policy outperforms a do-nothing policy; myopic
and rolling policies each respectively improve upon that baseline. As
expected based on the duck density projections, wet/dry scenario is a
larger determinant of improvement than RCP scenario.

Overall improvement of any acquisition policy, expressed in \% over
baseline, is relatively small (0.64\% at best). This is likely an
artifact of how conversion risk is being calculated in this model (a
measure of habitat erosion standing in for habitat conversion
probability). It may also be related to a relatively conservative budget
parameter (only \textasciitilde60-70 EAUs are acquired in most
scenarios). That said, these numbers might not be that different from
real world estimates: Kemnick et al 2023 estimated that the program
impact for SWAP was ``an avoided loss between 0.00\% and 0.02\% of the
carrying capacity for broods and breeding waterfowl from 2008--2017.''

\subsection{Acquisitions by policy: rolling vs
myopic}\label{acquisitions-by-policy-rolling-vs-myopic}

This map shows parcels that are acquired under both rolling and myopic
acquisition schedules; those that are acquired only under rolling; and
those that are acquired only under myopic. Light blue parcels are those
that are purchased only under an optimal acquisition policy; the myopic
model skips these parcels. The orange parcels are ones that the myopic
policy purchased but the rolling policy declined to purchase.
Interpretation here is that light blue parcels are where the rolling
policy's advantage comes from; orange is where the myopic dedicated
funds sub-optimally.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/foresight_difference.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-foresight}\textbf{Rolling vs.~myopic acquisition
sets.} EAUs colored by which policy buys them, under each climate
scenario. Purple cells are parcels acquired under both policies; blue
cells are purchased only under the rolling policies; orange cells are
purchased by the myopic policy but avoided by the rolling policy.}

\end{figure}%

\newpage{}

\section{Updates}\label{updates}

\subsubsection{10/26/2026: Conversion risk sensitivity
analysis}\label{conversion-risk-sensitivity-analysis}

In an effort to compare our model results to published estimates of
landscape-scale conversion rates for wetlands and grasslands, I re-ran
the model with conversion risk rescaled to different conversion rates.
The FORESce derived conversion risk data acts as a lower bound: the
landscape average of suitable-to-unsuitable habitat transition is
equivalent to a 0.17\% annual rate in 2020. Other published estimates
vary, ranging from 0.28\% (Johnston et al 2013) to 1.33\% (Rashford et
al.~2011).

I re-scaled all conversion risk values so that the mean landscape-wide
2020 conversion rate (i.e., the share of all unprotected suitable
habitat converted per decade) equaled the target rate. I calculated a
scaling factor, k, which would bring the mean landscape-scale conversion
rate to the literature-derived rate, then applied that scaling factor to
the conversion risk table in every EAU in every decade. This approach
multiplies the underlying conversion rate by the same factor in every
EAU, decade and scenario, so the spatial pattern and the trajectory of
conversion risk over time (including the mid-century collapse under
GCAM) are unchanged. Benefit, cost, budget and land cover are identical
to the original run.

We find that the overall pattern of rolling outperforms myopic which
outperforms greedy still holds. The performance gap, however, narrows.
Rather than \textasciitilde50\% improvement of rolling \textgreater{}
greedy, for example, we see \textasciitilde{} 35-40\% improvement. The
value of the rolling policy as compared to the myopic or greedy policy
declines as conversion risk rises.

\caption{\label{fig-hsw-pct}\textbf{Policy performance across conversion
rates (\% improvement).} Left: myopic vs.~greedy (value of using
conversion risk); middle: rolling vs.~myopic (value of foresight);
right: rolling vs.~greedy (total). Each tick gives the annual rate and
the estimate it stands for.}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/fig_haz_sweep_risk_box.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-hsw-risk}\textbf{Per-period conversion risk across
EAUs, by decade and emissions scenario.} Colour = annual conversion rate
of each run (FOREsce derived = lightest). Box = median and IQR; diamond
= mean; dash = maximum.}

\end{figure}%

In contrast to the landcover comparison section (below), we see here
that the trajectory of conversion risk is the same across all scaling
factors- only the magnitude of conversion risk changes.

\textbf{Data Sources:} Johnston et al 2013: Johnston, Carol A. ``Wetland
losses due to row crop expansion in the Dakota Prairie Pothole Region.''
Wetlands 33.1 (2013): 175-182. 0.28\% annual NWI wetland loss rate.

Stephens et al 20028: Stephens, Scott E., et al.~``Predicting risk of
habitat conversion in native temperate grasslands.'' Conservation
biology 22.5 (2008): 1320-1330. 0.4\% annualized probability of
conversion from grassland to cropland, 1989-2003

Kemnick et al 2023: Kemink, Kaylan M., et al.~``Assessing prioritization
measures for a private land conservation program in the US Prairie
Pothole Region.'' Conservation Science and Practice 5.7 (2023): e12939.
0.57\% estimated upper bound for wetland drainage rates.

Rashford et al 2011: Rashford, Benjamin S., Johann A. Walker, and
Christopher T. Bastian. ``Economics of grassland conversion to cropland
in the Prairie Pothole Region.'' Conservation Biology 25.2 (2011):
276-284.

\newpage{}

\subsubsection{10/21/2026: Budget sensitivity
analysis}\label{budget-sensitivity-analysis}

We wanted to explore the role of budget in determining the relatively
performance of the three acquisition policies, as well as the overall
performance over the do-nothing baseline. I re-ran the model with a
stricter budget constraint (2 median-priced EAUs acquired per period)
and a more generous budget constraint (10 median-priced EAUs per period)
under all climate scenarios, using the GCAM reference landcover
scenario.

I found that across climate scenarios, a stricter budget constraint
increased the performance gap between acquisition policies. When only 2
median-priced EAUs can be purchased per decision period, the rolling
policy outperforms greedy/myopic policy by \textasciitilde{} 75\%/25\%;
when 10 median-priced EAUs can be purchased per decision period, the
rolling policy outperforms the greedy/myopic policy by only
\textasciitilde{} 35\%/\textasciitilde15\%.

\textbf{The value of foresight increases under a stricter budget, and
declines under a more generous budget}.

\global\setlength{\arrayrulewidth}{\Oldarrayrulewidth}

\global\setlength{\tabcolsep}{\Oldtabcolsep}

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

\newpage{}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/fig_bud_overtime.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-bud-overtime}\textbf{Cumulative performance relative
to the rolling policy, by budget.} Discounted duck-pairs secured by the
greedy and myopic policies minus those secured by rolling, cumulative to
2100. Rows = climate scenario; columns = budget (median-priced EAUs per
period). Rolling is the benchmark (0).}

\end{figure}%

\newpage{}

As expected, I found the a more generous budget constraint resulted in a
higher conservation value over the do-nothing baseline. Note that even a
doubling of the budget from the original constraint of nEAU=5 to nEAU=10
only resulted in a \textasciitilde.6\% or .7\% improvement over the
do-nothing baseline- but note that actual pairs conserved increases
dramatically (in some cases more than tripling).

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/map_bud_foresight.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-bud-foresight}\textbf{Rolling vs.~myopic acquisition
sets, by budget.} EAUs colored by which policy buys them. Rows = climate
scenario; columns = budget (median-priced EAUs per period). Purple cells
are acquired under both policies; blue cells only under the rolling
policy; orange cells are bought by the myopic policy but avoided by the
rolling policy.}

\end{figure}%

In a stricter budget regime, acquisitions are concentrated in the
eastern PPR, where the most dense duck abundances are concentrated. In
the more generous budget regimes, more parcels are acquired and more
parcels in the western PPR are acquired, particularly under the rolling
policy.

\newpage{}

\subsubsection{10/21/2026: Land-cover scenario comparison (GCAM
reference
vs.~BAU)}\label{land-cover-scenario-comparison-gcam-reference-vs.-bau}

In order to explore the role of landcover trends and patterns on model
results, I re-ran the model with another FOREsce projection (BAU). Both
BAU and GCAM reference are considered to be likely scenarios in a world
with no major agricultural or climate policy changes (as per the FORESce
description):

\begin{itemize}
\item
  BAU: ``Based on an extrapolation of recent land-cover trends as
  derived from remote-sensing data. Overall trends were provided by 2001
  to 2011 change in the National Land Cover Database, while change in
  crop types were extrapolated from 2008 to 2014 change in the Cropland
  Data Layer. Overall the scenario is marked by expansion of high-value
  traditional crops (corn, soybeans, cotton), with a concurrent decline
  in dryland wheat and some other lower-value crops.''
\item
  GCAM: ``Based on global-scale scenarios from the GCAM model,
  the''reference'' scenario provides a likely landscape under a world
  without specific carbon or climate mitigation efforts. As such, it's
  another form of a ``business-as-usual'' scenario.''
\end{itemize}

I wanted to compare these to see how changes in landcover estimations
might affect model results, and specifically to see if the BAU scenario
contained higher conversion rates than the GCAM scenario; it was
noticeable in the GCAM scenario that conversion rates declined markedly
mid-century, and wanted to explore what effect that would have.

I found that the policy ranking (rolling outperforms myopic which
outperforms greedy) holds in both land-cover scenarios under both
landcover projections. While the ranking holds, the magnitude of the
performance gap varies between the landcover scenarios: the rolling
policy still outperforms greedy and myopic in the BAU, but by
substantially less than under the GCAM scenario.

\arrayrulecolor[HTML]{000000}

\global\setlength{\arrayrulewidth}{\Oldarrayrulewidth}

\global\setlength{\tabcolsep}{\Oldtabcolsep}

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

I believe this is driven by different patterns in conversion risk: under
GCAM, conversion to cropland is concentrated in the first two decades
and then flattens to nearly zero mid-century and never recovers. This
has two effects: the overall value of any acquisition policy through
2100 as compared to a do-nothing baseline is relatively low (since
conversion risk in most decades is near zero), and the greedy/myopic
policy significantly under perform the rolling policy in early decades
(since they assumes continued high conversion risk when in fact
conversion risk collapses).

Under BAU, by contrast, the conversion risk is relatively stable across
the entire study period. There is decadal variability, but there is no
consistent mid-century collapse. This results in a reversal of the two
effects: the overall value of any acquisition policy through 2100 as
compared to a do-nothing baseline is higher; and greedy/myopic policy
assumption of constant conversion risk is in fact pretty close to true.

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=1\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{runs/gcam_ref/output_figs/fig_cmp_risk_box.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cmp-risk}\textbf{Per-period conversion risk across
EAUs, by decade, emissions scenario and land-cover scenario. Note that
conversion risk collapses mid-century in GCAM, but stays constant in
BAU.} Grey = GCAM ref; red = BAU. Box = median and IQR; diamond = mean;
dash = maximum.}

\end{figure}%

\renewcommand*{\arraystretch}{1}

I think this comparison is an interesting way to show how the model can
help us understand the value of time-dependent acquisition policies in
different systems. My primary take away from this is that the trajectory
of conversion risk is more important than actual scale of conversion
risk for determining the value/utility of a time-dependent acquisition
policy as compared to a traditional (greedy/myopic) policy. I don't know
that we need to report BAU results alongside GCAM results, but perhaps
include the comparison as a sensitivity analysis in supplementary
materials?

\newpage{}

\subsubsection{9/30/2026: Protected Area
Removal}\label{protected-area-removal}

In examining the conversion risk, we realized that the previous
conversion risk included protected area in the total pool of potentially
available habitat, which artificially diluted the estimated conversion
risk. To address this, I overlayed the USGS Protected Area Database
(PADUS) and calculated the gross suitable habitat loss with protected
areas removed. This revised gross suitable habitat rate was used in the
risk of loss calculation (i.e., script 05); however, the other uses of
landcover data (such as distributing waterfowl density within a WMD)
continue to use the unmasked raster with protected areas included.

To overlay the PADUS layer, I used the PAD-US 4.1 Vector Analysis (USGS
GAP, 2024, PAD-US 4.1, https://doi.org/10.5066/P96WBCHS) and masked out
polygons marked as GAP Status Code 1-3 (which covers areas with
permanent protection, like National Parks, and areas where ``low
intensity'' land uses is allowed). I then created a 0/1 raster of
protected land within the PPR, and removed those areas when calculating
the gross habitat loss.

Updating the conversion risk to exclude protected areas increases the
comparative performance of the optimal model over the greedy model by
\textasciitilde3-4 percentage points; it's noticeable, but not a
fundamental change to the takeaway message.

\subsubsection{9/15/2026: Conversion Risk Update: Net vs Gross Habitat
loss:}\label{conversion-risk-update-net-vs-gross-habitat-loss}

Delving in to the calculations of how I quantified conversion risk, I
realized that I was using net rather than gross habitat loss. This was
not conceptually consistent with what I presented, which I intended to
be the proportion of suitable habitat in time t that was lost by time
t+1 (a flow). Instead, I had calculated the difference between the stock
of suitable habitat in time t - t+1, which reflects both losses and
gains in suitable habitat.

I changed the calculations to see going from net to gross would affect
the estimate of conversion risk. I found that there is a fair amount of
habitat recovery in the FOREsce models, so some of those habitat gains
showed in the net calculation were concealing real habitat losses. The
table below compares the net vs gross habitat loss rate (gross
\textgreater{} net in all decades but particularly mid-century). In
addition, the use of the net metric in the first model run meant that
some EAUs had a ``positive'' habitat loss (i.e., overall habitat gain),
which I corrected for by imposing a data-derived epsilon (floor). With
the gross metric, that is no longer needed (net loss \textgreater= 0).
The table also shows the percent of EAUs that were ``censored'' by the
artificial floor (i.e., which really had habitat loss but which was
hidden by habitat gain).

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lllll@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Decade & gross & net & \% of EAUs censored & \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
2020--2040 & \textasciitilde0.018 & \textasciitilde0.017 & 0.5--7\% & \\
\textbf{2050--2070 (4.5)} & \textasciitilde0.002 & 0.0009 &
\textbf{14--17\%} & \\
\textbf{2050--2070 (8.5)} & \textasciitilde0.002 & −0.0009 &
\textbf{16--24\%} & \\
2080--2090 & \textasciitilde0.003 & \textasciitilde0.002 &
\textasciitilde4\% & \\
\end{longtable}
}

While we were missing meaningful habitat loss by using the net metric,
changing to the gross metric didn't substantially change the overall
model results. When I used gross instead of net habitat loss, this
increased the performance of the rolling model over the greedy model by
\textasciitilde1-2 percentage points.

\subsubsection{7/30/2026: Spatial Join error}\label{spatial-join-error}

I discovered an error in the spatial join of the extracted land cover
data and the model's data panel. I realized that the landcover data was
joined to EAUs by row position, which introduced errors- land
classification (\% suitable habitat) was joined to the wrong EAUs.
Conversion risk (hazard) was the variable most affected- the trans\_prob
were not attached to the correct spatial unit. Because duck density is
distributed according to \% suitable, it also meant that duck
distributions were incorrect- but the scale of this error was mitigated
by the fact that WMD-scale determines the absolute amount of duck
density. This meant that WMD level abundance numbers are fine, but
distribution within WMD to EAUs was incorrect; cost numbers are
unaffected.

Because of this, I had incorrectly believed that most of the EAUs with
missing landcover data were concentrated in Winsom, which is why I
removed it- but I discovered that this wasn't actually the case. There
were in fact multiple WMDs with at least a few EAUs missing data. I
needed to make a cut off threshold (\% EAU with missing data above which
the WMD would be excluded); I decided to make a cap of 20\% (i.e., any
WMD missing 20\% of EAU land cover data would be excluded). This removed
one WMD (Fergus Falls), and left \textasciitilde8 WMDs with some small
\% of landcover data missing (2-13\%). Note that this means that in
those remaining WMDs, the full WMD duck numbers are more densely
concentrated in the remaining EAUs.

When I fixed this, I saw that the spatial distribution of duck abundance
is now more clearly aligned with McKenna 2026 (good news!). Compare Fig.
1 with Fig. 1 from ``results\_07302026''. There is also now a clearer
alignment of duck abundance and LOW acquisition cost: this is more
consistent with the literature, which regularly finds that the highest
duck abundance is in the western PPR where land prices are also lower.
But the major model findings don't change at all (i.e., rolling
outperforms both myopic and greedy, overall acquisition rate remains
low)

\newpage{}

\subsection{7/10/2026: Temporal alignment of benefit
data}\label{temporal-alignment-of-benefit-data}

After the initial model run, I realized there was a problem with the
temporal alignment of the benefit data with the landcover data (and the
decision model itself). The benefit data is as follows:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  ``Obs'' = mean 2007--2017
\item
  ``3059'' = mean 2030--2059
\item
  ``7099'' = mean 2070--2099
\end{itemize}

Originally, I had aligned it as follows:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  ``Obs'' = mean 2007--2017 -\textgreater{} 2020
\item
  ``3059'' = mean 2030--2059 -\textgreater{} 2050
\item
  ``7099'' = mean 2070--2099 -\textgreater{} 2100
\end{itemize}

\begin{description}
\tightlist
\item[After talking with Owen, I updated the alignment to use window
midpoints]
\end{description}

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  ``Obs'' = mean 2007--2017 -\textgreater{} 2012
\item
  ``3059'' = mean 2030--2059 -\textgreater{} 2045
\item
  ``7099'' = mean 2070--2099 -\textgreater{} 285
\end{itemize}

Notes: The 2020 baseline is produced by interpolating each RCP×GCM combo
on its 2012→2045 segment and then averaging across the four combos into
one shared period-0 row (preserves the shared-baseline used for the 2020
risk). Decades 2030/2040 interpolate on the 2012→2045 segment;
2050--2080 on the 2045→2085 segment; 2090 and 2100 are held constant at
the 2085 value

I re-ran the model with this midpoint temporal alignment of benefit
data; saw little difference in model results. My takeaway is that we can
reasonably use this midpoint alignment, and importantly that it doesn't
much matter for model results.
